Description
Pattern matching rule 'generic-password-assignment' (Hardcoded password assignment) was found in this file. (Extracted from embedded strings inside a PE binary.)
Remediation
Remove hardcoded credentials/secrets from source and configuration files. Use a secrets manager (Azure Key Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, HashiCorp Vault) or environment variables injected at runtime, and rotate the exposed credential immediately.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Extract printable strings from the binary directly to confirm independently:
   Windows (Sysinternals strings.exe):
     strings64.exe -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\ACRT010.dll" | findstr /i "Password"
   Linux:
     strings -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\ACRT010.dll" | grep -i 'Password'

2. Open ACRT010.dll in a PE viewer (CFF Explorer, Detect It Easy, or IDA/Ghidra) and locate the string in the resource/data section to confirm it is not dead code or a leftover debug string.

3. If confirmed live, treat this the same as a config-file secret: rotate the credential and remove it from the compiled binary — hardcoded strings in compiled code are trivially recoverable with basic reverse-engineering tools regardless of packing.

Description
Pattern matching rule 'odbc-pwd' (ODBC/OLEDB connection string password) was found in this file. (Extracted from embedded strings inside a PE binary.)
Remediation
Remove hardcoded credentials/secrets from source and configuration files. Use a secrets manager (Azure Key Vault, AWS Secrets Manager, HashiCorp Vault) or environment variables injected at runtime, and rotate the exposed credential immediately.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)

     strings64.exe -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\ASEMBLY.dll" | findstr /i "PWD=
 AS"
   Linux:
     strings -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\ASEMBLY.dll" | grep -i 'PWD=
 AS'

2. Open ASEMBLY.dll in a PE viewer (CFF Explorer, Detect It Easy, or IDA/Ghidra) and locate the string in the resource/data section to confirm it is not dead code or a leftover debug string.

3. If confirmed live, treat this the same as a config-file secret: rotate the credential and remove it from the compiled binary — hardcoded strings in compiled code are trivially recoverable with basic reverse-engineering tools regardless of packing.

Evidence
Imports 'MSVBVM60.DLL', not found in app directory tree or known-system-DLL list
Description
'MSVBVM60.DLL' is imported by this binary but is neither a recognized Windows system DLL nor present anywhere in the scanned application directory. If this DLL is genuinely missing at runtime, the default DLL search order will let an attacker plant a malicious DLL with this name in a searched directory (application directory, current working directory, or PATH) to achieve code execution in this process's context.
Remediation
Confirm whether this DLL is expected to be present on the target system. If so, verify it's installed with correct permissions from a trusted source; if it's an optional/plugin dependency, load it via a fully-qualified path rather than relying on search order.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Confirm the import is genuinely unresolved on a real target install (not just missing from this static analysis machine):
     Copy the application to a clean Windows VM matching the target's OS build, then run:
     Process Monitor (procmon.exe) filtered to Process Name = "CMQ130.exe", Operation = "Load Image", and look for a "NAME NOT FOUND" result for 'MSVBVM60.DLL'.

2. If confirmed missing, build a minimal proof-of-concept DLL named exactly 'MSVBVM60.DLL' exporting a DllMain that writes a marker to a log file (e.g. using a template PoC DLL project or `x86_64-w64-mingw32-gcc -shared -o MSVBVM60.DLL poc.c`).

3. Place the PoC DLL in a directory searched before the location where the real DLL (if any) would legitimately reside — typically the application's own directory or the process's current working directory, per the default DLL search order.

4. Launch 'CMQ130.exe' and confirm the PoC DLL's DllMain executed (check the marker log file) to demonstrate code execution in this process's context via the missing dependency.

dll-hijackphantom-dll
●Medium Dynamic library loading without safe search-order controls 1 affected file
rule: dll-search-order-exposure · confidence: Medium
Affected Files
C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\regsvr32.dll
Evidence
Imports LoadLibrary(A/W) but no SetDllDirectory/SetDefaultDllDirectories/AddDllDirectory call detected
Description
This binary dynamically loads libraries at runtime but shows no evidence of restricting the DLL search order (e.g. via SetDllDirectory or SetDefaultDllDirectories). By default, Windows' DLL search order can include the current working directory and other attacker-influenceable locations ahead of System32, depending on how the DLL is loaded and OS version/mitigations.
Remediation
Call SetDefaultDllDirectories(LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_SYSTEM32) early in the application, use fully-qualified paths when loading DLLs, and avoid loading libraries by bare name from an attacker-writable working directory.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps
1. Confirm the imports directly with a PE inspection tool:
     Windows: dumpbin /imports "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\regsvr32.dll"   (or open in CFF Explorer / PE-bear)
     Linux:   python3 -c "import pefile; pe=pefile.PE(r'C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\regsvr32.dll'); [print(e.dll) for e in pe.DIRECTORY_ENTRY_IMPORT]"
   Confirm LoadLibrary(A/W) is imported and no SetDllDirectory/SetDefaultDllDirectories/AddDllDirectory call is present.

2. Identify a DLL this binary loads by bare name (via Process Monitor with a 'Load Image' filter while running the binary from a writable working directory):
     procmon.exe  (filter: Process Name is "regsvr32.dll", Operation is "Load Image", Result is "NAME NOT FOUND")

3. For any DLL Procmon shows being searched for in the current working directory (and not found there), place a proof-of-concept DLL with that exact name in the working directory used to launch the application. A minimal PoC DLL just needs a DllMain that writes to a log file on load.

4. Re-run the application from that working directory and confirm the planted DLL's DllMain executed (check the PoC log file), demonstrating arbitrary code execution in this process's context via search-order hijacking.

Evidence
No Authenticode signature (security directory) present
Description
This binary carries no Authenticode signature, so its origin and integrity cannot be cryptographically verified. Unsigned binaries are easier to tamper with or substitute without detection, and are often flagged by endpoint security and SmartScreen.
Remediation
Sign all shipped executables and libraries with a valid code-signing certificate from a trusted CA, and verify signatures as part of the build/release pipeline.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Confirm no signature is present directly:
     Windows (PowerShell):
       Get-AuthenticodeSignature -FilePath "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMQ130.exe" | Format-List
       (Status will show 'NotSigned')
     Linux:
       osslsigncode verify "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMQ130.exe"
       (Output will show 'No signature found')

2. Impact demonstration: because there is no signature, Windows SmartScreen/AppLocker/WDAC publisher-based rules cannot distinguish this binary from a tampered or malicious replacement. If this file sits in a writable location (cross-reference any writable-directory finding for the same path), an attacker's replacement binary would be indistinguishable from the original at the OS trust level.

3. For the client report: capture the Get-AuthenticodeSignature output as evidence alongside a screenshot of the file's Properties > Digital Signatures tab in Windows Explorer (which will show no 'Digital Signatures' tab at all for an unsigned file).

Evidence
Update-related string matched ('update'); no WinVerifyTrust/CryptQueryObject-style import found
Description
This binary contains strings suggesting an update/version-check mechanism, but does not import any Windows API commonly used to verify a downloaded payload's signature before execution. This is a static heuristic, not proof of an exploitable auto-update flaw — confirm manually by reviewing the update flow (ideally with source or a decompiler).
Remediation
Ensure any auto-update mechanism verifies the downloaded payload's Authenticode signature (e.g. via WinVerifyTrust) and expected publisher before executing it, and serves updates over TLS with certificate validation enabled.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Confirm the update-related string directly:
     strings -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMST310.exe" | grep -i 'update'
   (Windows: strings64.exe -n 6 "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMST310.exe" | findstr /i "update")

2. Confirm the import table lacks trust-verification APIs:
     dumpbin /imports "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMST310.exe" | findstr /i "WinVerifyTrust CryptQueryObject"
   (expect no output if this finding is accurate)

3. Dynamic confirmation (requires a controlled test environment and, if the update server is client-owned, written authorization): intercept the update check/download with a proxy (Burp/mitmproxy) and serve a modified payload with a valid file structure but altered content. If the application executes the modified payload without rejecting it, this confirms the updater does not verify payload integrity/authenticity before execution.

4. If dynamic testing is out of scope for this engagement, report this as a static indicator requiring source-level or decompiled confirmation of the actual update-verification logic before treating it as a confirmed (rather than potential) finding.

Evidence
OPTIONAL_HEADER.DllCharacteristics missing IMAGE_DLLCHARACTERISTICS_DYNAMIC_BASE
Description
This binary was not compiled with /DYNAMICBASE, so its modules load at a predictable base address on every run. This significantly simplifies memory-corruption exploitation (return-to-libc, ROP chain construction) by removing the need to defeat address randomization.
Remediation
Recompile with the /DYNAMICBASE linker flag (enabled by default in modern MSVC; verify it hasn't been explicitly disabled).
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Confirm directly with PowerShell:
     Import-Module .\Get-PESecurity.psm1
     Get-PESecurity -file "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMQ130.exe"
   (ASLR column will show False/Disabled)

2. Or inspect the raw flag with pefile:
     python3 -c "import pefile; pe=pefile.PE(r'C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMQ130.exe'); print(bool(pe.OPTIONAL_HEADER.DllCharacteristics & 0x40))"

3. Demonstrate impact: run the binary twice and compare its module base address in Process Hacker/System Informer (Properties > Memory) or via WinDbg's `lm` command. Identical base addresses across runs confirm ASLR is not in effect for this module, which is a precondition for reliable memory-corruption exploit development.

Evidence
OPTIONAL_HEADER.DllCharacteristics missing IMAGE_DLLCHARACTERISTICS_NX_COMPAT
Description
This binary was not compiled with /NXCOMPAT, meaning it opts out of hardware DEP support. Combined with a memory-corruption bug, this makes classic shellcode injection into a data segment (stack/heap) directly executable.
Remediation
Recompile with the /NXCOMPAT linker flag (default in modern MSVC).
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps (example — first affected file shown; repeat against each file listed above)
1. Confirm directly:
     python3 -c "import pefile; pe=pefile.PE(r'C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\CMQ130.exe'); print(bool(pe.OPTIONAL_HEADER.DllCharacteristics & 0x100))"

2. Cross-check at runtime in Process Hacker/System Informer: select the running process > Properties > General tab > 'DEP' should show 'Disabled' if the flag is truly not honored (Windows may still enforce system-wide DEP depending on policy — note both the binary flag and the runtime enforcement outcome as separate data points).

Evidence
No populated SEHandlerTable in load config directory, or IMAGE_DLLCHARACTERISTICS_NO_SEH set
Description
This 32-bit binary does not appear to have SafeSEH enabled, leaving structured exception handler chains unvalidated. Exploits that overwrite an SEH record can redirect execution without SafeSEH validating that the handler address is a registered, expected one.
Remediation
Recompile with the /SAFESEH linker flag (32-bit only; not applicable/needed for 64-bit targets, which use table-based SEH).

Description
This binary was not compiled with /GUARD:CF, so indirect call targets are not validated at runtime. This makes ROP/JOP-style control-flow hijacking following a memory-corruption bug comparatively easier to pull off.
Remediation

rule: embedded-pdb-path-leak · confidence: High
Affected Files
C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\IDSTEMP\wordpad.exe
Evidence
PDB path: wordpad.pdb
Description
This binary embeds a full path to its PDB debug symbol file, typically left over from an unstripped release build. This commonly leaks internal developer machine usernames, build server directory structure, or internal project naming conventions — minor but free reconnaissance information for an attacker profiling the target organization's build environment.
Remediation
Strip debug directory entries from release builds, or use /PDBALTPATH with a generic relative path so no internal filesystem information is embedded in shipped binaries.
Proof of Concept / Reproduction Steps
1. Confirm directly:
     dumpbin /headers "C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\IDSTEMP\wordpad.exe" | findstr /i pdb
   or with pefile:
     python3 -c "import pefile; pe=pefile.PE(r'C:\Users\ankes\Downloads\Securis360\Clients\CAMA\Next70Client\Next70Client\IDSTEMP\wordpad.exe'); pe.parse_data_directories(); print(pe.DIRECTORY_ENTRY_DEBUG[0].entry.PdbFileName)"

2. Review the leaked path for anything organizationally sensitive: 'wordpad.pdb' — note the OS username, drive layout, and any internal project/codename it reveals for the client report.

Description
This binary shows no evidence of anti-debugging checks. This is an exposure/maturity signal only — it means the binary can likely be attached to and analyzed with a standard debugger (x64dbg, WinDbg) without needing to defeat any anti-debug trick, which lowers the effort required for dynamic reverse engineering. This is informational, not a vulnerability in itself, and anti-debug measures are not a substitute for proper security controls.
Remediation
If runtime tamper-resistance is a business requirement for this binary (e.g. anti-cheat, DRM, licensing enforcement), consider standard anti-debug/anti-tamper techniques as one layer of defense — but do not rely on them as a primary security control, since they only raise analysis cost rather than prevent it.
